CAPITAL STRUCTURE 
3 
Q. 
Can you describe what a capital structure 
4 
consists of? 
5 
A. 
The capital structure is the mixture of types of 
6 
capital, such as debt and equity, that a company 
7 
uses to finance its business; in the case of a 
8 
utility company, it is the capital deployed to 
9 
support its rate base.     
10 
Q. 
Please describe a holding company’s capital 
11 
structure in general. 
12 
A. 
A utility holding company’s capital structure is 
13 
essentially the sum of its subsidiary companies, 
14 
which may have differing capital structures 
15 
depending on the type of businesses they are 
16 
engaged in and the perceived risks associated 
17 
with those businesses.  These subsidiaries may 
18 
be regulated operating companies, lightly 
19 
regulated transmission companies, competitive 
20 
and renewable generation companies, and 
21 
occasionally even non-utility related entities.  
22 
These subsidiaries may be located in multiple 
23 
jurisdictions and even multiple countries.   
24 
2350


--- Page 367 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
15 
 
Q. 
How does a utility holding company report its 
1 
overall capital structure? 
2 
A. 
A utility holding company reports its overall 
3 
capital structure as part of its consolidated 
4 
balance sheet in various filings, as well as in 
5 
its annual and quarterly reports to 
6 
shareholders.  The consolidated balance sheet 
7 
reflects the financial position of all the 
8 
holding company's operations. 
9 
Q. 
Are there different types of capital structure 
10 
approaches that can be considered when trying to 
11 
determine an appropriate capital structure for 
12 
an operating company that is part of a larger 
13 
holding company? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  In general, there are three approaches 
15 
that can be utilized when determining the 
16 
appropriate capital structure for an operating 
17 
company.  If the operating company is adequately 
18 
ring-fenced and issues its own debt, then a 
19 
stand-alone capital structure could be used, 
20 
provided that the operating company is 
21 
capitalized in a manner that is cost effective 
22 
and allows it to maintain reasonable access to 
23 
capital.  Absent adequate ring-fencing, the 
24 
2351


--- Page 368 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
16 
 
capital structure of the parent company, or an 
1 
intermediary company between the parent and 
2 
operating company, could be considered.  Again, 
3 
this would depend upon the reasonableness of 
4 
that capital structure in terms of allowing the 
5 
operating company to maintain reasonable access 
6 
to capital.  If neither of these circumstances 
7 
exist, a hypothetical capital structure based 
8 
upon similarly situated distribution utility 
9 
companies could be used. 
10 
Q.  Please describe what you mean by ring-fencing.  
11 
A. 
Ring-fencing describes situations where legal 
12 
and operational mechanisms have been deployed to 
13 
insulate the utility from the potential risks 
14 
posed by the parent’s riskier, non-regulated 
15 
activities.  Adequate ring-fencing is generally 
16 
achieved by restricting the shifting of assets 
17 
and liabilities between the parent and 
18 
subsidiary through the use of restrictive 
19 
covenants.  In addition to restrictive 
20 
covenants, two other mechanisms, a golden share 
21 
and a non-consolidation opinion, have been found 
22 
to be effective by the rating agencies.  In 
23 
theory, if a utility subsidiary is adequately 
24 
2352


--- Page 369 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
17 
 
ring-fenced, in the event the holding company or 
1 
another subsidiary goes bankrupt, creditors 
2 
cannot attach their claims to the assets of the 
3 
ring-fenced subsidiary and vice versa.  
4 
Effective ring-fencing mechanisms will generally 
5 
result in at least one to three notches 
6 
differential in the ratings of the regulated 
7 
utility and its diversified parent.  
8 
Q. 
Do you believe that the use of a stand-alone 
9 
capital structure for Central Hudson is 
10 
appropriate to use for ratemaking purposes in 
11 
this proceeding? 
12 
A. 
Yes, I do.   
13

14

Q.

18

19 
A. 
There are three reasons why it is incorrect to 
20 
use the operating company data.  First, the 
21 
operating company’s common equity balance may be 
22

23

2365


--- Page 382 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419

2 
equity on the operating company’s books.  
3 
Because of this, the operating company’s equity 
4 
is really debt issued at another level in the 
5 
company. 
6

24 
2366


--- Page 383 ---

18

19

Q. 
What is the second reason it is incorrect to use 
5

6 
A. 
The second reason it is incorrect to use the 
7

8 
not in customers’ interests to pay for common 
9 
equity ratios that are higher than the common 
10 
equity ratio of the parent company.  Rating 
11 
agencies, in whole or in part, base their 
12

13 
structure, absent suitable ring-fencing.  Under 
14 
these circumstances, there is no reason to pay 
15 
for additional equity because it will not enable 
16 
the utility to achieve a higher credit rating or 
17 
realize lower borrowing costs.   
18

19

21

2368

11

18

How did you determine that the Company’s credit 
19

20 
A. 
I based my analysis on the credit metrics used 
21 
by S&P and Moody’s when evaluating the financial 
22 
and business risks of regulated electric and gas 
23 
utilities.  
24 
2374


--- Page 391 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
39 
 
Q. 
Please discuss the concept of financial risk. 
1 
A. 
Financial risk is the additional risk a 
2 
shareholder bears when a company uses debt in 
3 
addition to equity financing.  Companies that 
4 
employ greater leverage have higher financial 
5 
risk than companies financed mostly or entirely 
6 
by equity. 
7 
Q. 
How does S&P assess financial risk for a 
8 
regulated utility?   
9 
A. 
S&P assesses financial risk for a regulated 
10 
utility by using distinct financial risk 
11 
profiles that descend from “Minimal,” for 
12 
companies with little to no debt on their 
13 
balance sheets, to “Highly Leveraged” for 
14 
companies financed very aggressively with debt.  
15 
Specifically, the individual categories are 
16 
“Minimal,” “Modest,” “Intermediate,” 
17 
“Significant,” “Aggressive,” and “Highly 
18 
Leveraged.”  The placement of a company in one 
19 
of these categories is based on the calculation 
20 
of seven different financial metric ratios.  The 
21 
S&P Corporate Rating Methodology, which outlines 
22 
these financial metric ratios, originally 
23 
published on November 19, 2013, is included as 
24 
2375


--- Page 392 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
40 
 
Exhibit___(ASH-14). 
1 
Q. 
What is S&P’s current assessment of Central 
2 
Hudson’s financial risk? 
3 
A. 
S&P currently assesses Central Hudson’s 
4 
financial risk as “Aggressive.” 
5 
Q. 
What are the financial metric ratios S&P uses to 
6 
assess financial risk? 
7 
A. 
As illustrated on pages 33 and 34 of S&P’s 
8 
Corporate Rating Methodology, S&P calculates two 
9 
core coverage ratios and five supplemental 
10 
coverage ratios.  Specifically, S&P examines the 
11 
following financial metric ratios in assessing a 
12 
firm’s credit risk: (a) Funds from Operations to 
13 
Debt (FFO/Debt), which is a core metric; (b) 
14 
Debt/Earnings before interest, taxes, 
15 
depreciation, and amortization (Debt to EBITDA), 
16 
which is a core metric; (c) Cash Flow from 
17 
Operations (CFO)/debt, which is a supplemental 
18 
metric; (d) Free Operating Cash Flow 
19 
(FOCF)/debt, which is a supplemental metric; (e) 
20 
Discretionary Cash Flow/debt, which is a 
21 
supplemental metric; (f) (FFO + interest)/cash 
22 
interest (FFO cash interest coverage), which is 
23 
a supplemental metric; and, (g) EBITDA/interest, 
24 
2376


--- Page 393 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
41 
 
which is a supplemental metric.  
1 
Q. 
What benchmark table does S&P use in its 
2 
assessment of Central Hudson? 
3 
A. 
S&P assesses Central Hudson’s financial metrics 
4 
using their medial volatility table, which 
5 
reflects Central Hudson’s lower-risk regulated 
6 
utility operations. 
7 
Q. 
Please discuss the concept of business risk.   
8 
A. 
Business risk is the risk inherent in a 
9 
company’s operations and reflects the risk that 
10 
it will fail to achieve its expected financial 
11 
performance.  It is affected by factors such as 
12 
a company’s sensitivity to the overall economy, 
13 
the level of competition it faces, and the 
14 
diversity of its customer and/or supplier bases. 
15 
Q. 
How does S&P assess business risk? 
16 
A. 
S&P’s business risk profile analysis 
17 
incorporates such factors as country risk, 
18 
industry risk, regulatory environment, company 
19 
competitive position, business and geographic 
20 
diversification, and management strategy.  In 
21 
descending order, S&P’s six business risk 
22 
profile categories range from “Excellent,” for 
23 
companies with relatively little business risk, 
24 
2377


--- Page 394 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
42 
 
to “Vulnerable” for companies with extremely 
1 
high levels of business risk.  Specifically, the 
2 
individual categories are “Excellent,” “Strong,” 
3 
“Satisfactory,” “Fair,” “Weak,” and 
4 
“Vulnerable.”  
5 
Q. 
What is S&P’s assessment of Central Hudson’s 
6 
business risk profile? 
7 
A. 
In determining the “BBB+” credit rating for 
8 
Central Hudson, S&P has assigned a business risk 
9 
profile of “Excellent” to the Company.

Q.  Do you agree with the methodology used by

1 
Q.  Why do you recommend the use of the most 
2 
 
recent actual U.S. Treasury yields in 
3 
calculating the cost rates for the projected new 
4 
issuances? 
5 
A.  The reason I recommend the use of current 
6 
 
U.S. Treasury yields is because movements in 
7 
 
long-term interest rates are difficult to 
8 
 
forecast.  Such forecasts are not only poor 
9 
 
predictors of the magnitude of the expected 
10 
 
change in interest rates, they are generally not  
11 
 
reliable with respect to the direction of the 
12 
 
change.  The best estimate of future long-term 
13 
interest rates is a no-change forecast, also 
14 
known as a “random-walk” forecast or, in other 
15 
words, the current rates of these debt 
16 
instruments.  For this reason, I believe current 
17 
U.S. Treasury yields should be used in 
18 
estimating future debt issuances. 
19 
Q. 
Do you have any evidence supporting the use of 
20 
current actual Treasury yields?   
21 
A. 
Yes.  This is discussed in a study entitled, “On 
22 
Forecasting Long-Term Interest Rates: Is the 
23 
Success of the No-Change Prediction Surprising?” 
24 
2398


--- Page 415 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
63 
 
by Dr. James E. Pesando, in the Journal of 
1 
Finance, September 1980, included as 
2 
Exhibit___(ASH-17).  In this study, James 
3 
Pesando concludes that “short-run movements in 
4 
long-term interest rates, quite simply, are not 
5 
likely to be forecastable.” 
6 
Q. 
Have any other experts made similar findings? 
7 
A. 
Yes.  This finding was also published in another 
8 
study entitled, “Just How Bad are Economists at 
9 
Predicting Interest Rates? (And What are the 
10 
Implications for Investors?)” by Kevin 
11 
Stephenson, in the Journal of Investing, Summer 
12 
1997, included as Exhibit___(ASH-18).  
13 
Stephenson evaluates economist’s predictions of 
14 
Treasury rates six months in advance during the 
15 
study period of 1982 to 1996.  He found that the 
16 
rate of return on the three-month Treasury Bill 
17 
moved in the opposite direction relative to the 
18 
consensus forecast 53 percent of the time, and 
19 
that the rate of return on the 30-year Treasury 
20 
Bond moved in the opposite direction relative to 
21 
the consensus forecast 67 percent of the time.   
22 
 
 
In addition, a similar study entitled, 
23 
“Professional Forecasts of Interest Rates and 
24 
2399


--- Page 416 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419

6

USE OF PROXY GROUP 
4 
Q. 
Why did you use a proxy group to estimate the 
5 
cost of equity for Central Hudson? 
6 
A. 
The use of a proxy group to determine the cost 
7 
of equity is necessary for several reasons.  
8 
First, the Company’s common stock is not 
9 
publicly traded, thus, direct DCF and CAPM 
10 
analyses of the Company are not possible.  
11 
Equally important is that DCF analyses for 
12 
individual companies rely on equity analysts’ 
13 
estimates of growth, and those estimates could 
14 
be biased or potentially inaccurate.  Similarly, 
15 
beta determinations used in the CAPM methodology 
16 
are based on historical observations that, due 
17 
to circumstances such as corporate 
18 
restructurings or major industry 
19 
transformations, may not be representative of 
20 
the level of earnings volatility expected in the 
21 
future.  By employing a sufficiently large proxy 
22 
group of similarly situated companies in our 
23 
analyses, I can minimize the undesirable effects 
24 
2407


--- Page 424 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
72 
 
of bias, both upward and downward, or inaccurate 
1 
estimates for any one company. 
2 
Q. 
What are the most important considerations for 
3 
selecting a proxy group? 
4 
A. 
First, it is important to determine the specific 
5 
industry classification of the subject company 
6 
to identify its true peers.  Second, once the 
7 
appropriate group of peer companies is 
8 
established, careful consideration must be given 
9 
to determining the appropriate screening 
10 
criteria to establish a group of companies that 
11 
is both sufficiently large and of similar risk 
12 
to the company in question.  A careful balance 
13 
must be struck between these two potentially 
14 
conflicting goals.  While the objective is to 
15 
select a group of companies whose risks closely 
16 
match those of the subject company, it is also 
17 
important that the proxy group be large enough 
18 
to provide sufficient confidence in the results.  
19 
The greater the number of suitable companies 
20 
that can be found, the less sensitive the 
21 
overall cost of equity estimate will be to 
22 
fluctuations or irregularities in the data 
23 
related to any one particular company. 
24 
2408


--- Page 425 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
73

Please describe how you selected your proxy 
1

2

I selected a group of 26 utility holding 
3 
companies from a “universe” of 38 holding 
4 
companies whose stock is publicly traded, all of 
5 
which are classified by Value Line as “electric 
6 
utilities.”  Due to the robust size of my proxy 
7 
group, I am confident that it will produce a 
8 
reliable estimate of the Company’s cost of 
9 
equity.  I have carefully selected companies 
10 
that face risks similar to those faced by 
11 
Central Hudson.  Illustrated in Exhibit___(ASH-
12

13

14 
assigned to each by S&P and Moody’s, accompanied 
15

16

17

18 
pages 2 and 3 of this exhibit, I portray the 
19 
same statistics for the entire Value Line 
20 
universe of companies.

22

DISCOUNTED CASH FLOW METHODOLOGY 
22 
Q. 
Please explain the basic theory underlying the 
23 
DCF methodology. 
24 
2413


--- Page 430 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
78 
 
A. 
The DCF approach can be applied to any 
1 
investment instrument that has an intrinsic 
2 
value.  The DCF approach, as it relates to 
3 
common stock, recognizes that companies create 
4 
value for their stockholders by using their 
5 
earnings in a number of ways, the most important 
6 
of which, by far, is through the payment of cash 
7 
dividends.  Alternatively, earnings that are 
8 
retained by companies can be used to create 
9 
value by investing in capital projects designed 
10 
to increase future profits.  The retained 
11 
earnings can also create value by retiring debt, 
12 
which reduces interest expense, thereby 
13 
resulting in greater cash flow available to 
14 
stockholders, and by buying back some of the 
15 
company’s common stock, which increases future 
16 
earnings on a per share basis. 
17 
Q. 
Describe the relationship between the value of a 
18 
company’s common stock and that of its future 
19 
dividends. 
20 
A. 
It is important to note that, while earnings 
21 
drive companies’ dividend payout policies, the 
22 
value of the companies’ common stock is always 
23 
equal to the present value of all future 
24 
2414


--- Page 431 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419

dividends.  This is because the earnings that 
1 
are retained will only have value to 
2

3

4 
principle is the strong assumption in capital 
5

6

7

8 
Q. 
Please explain why, according to DCF theory, 
9 
stocks have value. 
10 
A. 
The DCF theory assures us that stocks only have 
11 
value because of the cash flows that current 
12 
investors receive or the anticipation of cash 
13 
flows that future investors hope to receive.  
14 
Also, fundamental to the DCF methodology is the 
15 
notion that cash in the future is not worth as 
16 
much as cash today.  Due to reasons such as the 
17 
preference of individuals to consume today 
18 
rather than in the future, and because of 
19 
effects of expected inflation and productivity 
20 
on expected future cash flows, the DCF discounts 
21 
the future expected cash flows according to 
22 
investors return requirements. 
23 
Q. 
Why do you place primary reliance on the results 
24 
2415


--- Page 432 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
80 
 
of the DCF methodology? 
1 
A. 
The main reason the DCF methodology continues to 
2 
be the preferred approach for determining a 
3 
company’s cost of equity is that investors’ 
4 
immediate return requirements, as observed in 
5 
current stock prices and recent dividends, are 
6 
readily quantifiable.  The other principal 
7 
methodology, the CAPM, only relies tangentially, 
8 
through the use of utility beta values, upon 
9 
direct observations of actual utility investor 
10 
behavior.  The primary challenge in applying the 
11 
DCF is determining the rate of growth in future 
12 
dividends that investors expect. 
13 
Q. 
Can that growth rate be reliably projected with 
14 
respect to the utility industry? 
15 
A. 
Given the relatively mature and stable nature of 
16 
the utility industry, such estimates can be 
17 
derived with a reasonable degree of certitude.  
18 
Also, rational utility investors expect the 
19 
growth in future dividends to generally track 
20 
the changes in output, or growth in the overall 
21 
economy, as measured by growth in the nominal 
22 
Gross Domestic Product, referred to as GDP. 
23 
 
Moreover, just as nominal GDP growth 
24 
2416


--- Page 433 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
81 
 
incorporates gains achieved through the 
1 
application of new technologies, otherwise known 
2 
as productivity, and the effects of changes in 
3 
price levels, the investors’ growth 
4 
expectations, too, will reflect assumptions 
5 
regarding productivity and the rate of 
6 
inflation.  Consequently, when practiced with 
7 
the application of well-reasoned growth rate 
8 
estimates, such as those used in our approach, 
9 
the intuitiveness of the DCF methodology is 
10 
abundantly clear. 
11 
Q.

12

13

14

The DCF methodology’s intuitiveness is a primary 
15

16

23

24 
2417

Hale

requirements.  While the DCF has one input of 
1 
primary controversy (growth), two CAPM inputs 
2 
(beta and the market risk premium) are dependent 
3

4 
Q. 
Please describe your DCF methodology. 
5 
A. 
My DCF model is a two-stage “dividend discount” 
6 
model that is consistent with the financial 
7 
theory that the value of a company’s stock is 
8 
equivalent to its future cash flows.  My 
9 
dividend discount model forecasts those cash 
10 
flows into the future and discounts them back to 
11 
their present value.  It also explicitly 
12 
recognizes that a company’s short-term growth 
13 
expectations do not necessarily equal long-term 
14 
expectations; hence, my use of a two-stage 
15 
model.  Such a model is preferred, especially 
16 
when growth rates in the short-term and long-
17 
term might reasonably be expected to diverge, 
18 
thus making it superior to a single stage DCF 
19 
model, which assumes constant growth.  
20 
Q. 
How did you apply this model to the companies in 
21 
your proxy group? 
22 
A. 
For each company in the proxy group, a three-
23 
month average stock price was calculated by 
24 
2418


--- Page 435 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
83 
 
averaging the high and low price in each month 
1 
for the three-month period ended September 2023.  
2 
The model also contains Value Line data for 
3 
earnings per share, dividends per share, book 
4 
value per share and the forecasted amount of 
5 
outstanding common stock for each company.  
6 
 
This data is used to estimate the future 
7 
dividend payments that investors are currently 
8 
willing to pay for that future stream of 
9 
dividends, represented in this instance by the 
10 
average stock price taken over the three-month 
11 
period ended September 2023, which is 
12 
essentially the present value of those expected 
13 
dividends.  By calculating the discount rate 
14 
required to turn the string of expected dividend 
15 
payments into the current stock prices, I 
16 
determined the rates of return that investors 
17 
expect for each company.  The calculation of the 
18 
DCF for my proxy group is shown on pages 1 and 2 
19 
of Exhibit___(ASH-21).   
20 
Q. 
How are dividends projected to change over time?  
21 
A. 
My analysis maintains the same approach that

value of the 200-year dividend stream for each

4

Q. 
Please explain what you mean by sustainable 
6 
growth rate. 
7 
A. 
The sustainable growth rate is commonly viewed 
8 
as the maximum growth rate an enterprise can 
9 
achieve while maintaining a constant debt to 
10 
equity ratio; i.e., without having to increase 
11 
its financial leverage.  As illustrated in 
12 
Exhibit___(ASH-21), the growth forecast is 
13 
calculated for each proxy group company in 
14 
column “S” and the share growth estimate can be 
15 
found in column “R.”  The product of the growth 
16 
forecast and the share growth estimate, labeled 
17 
as column “V,” is used to calculate and 
18 
establish the sustainable growth rate.

7

8 
 
CAPITAL ASSET PRICING MODEL 
9 
Q. 
Please describe the basic theory underlying the 
10 
CAPM. 
11 
A. 
The principle behind the CAPM theory is that 
12 
there is no premium, in terms of expected 
13 
return, for bearing risks that can be eliminated 
14 
through diversification.  According to the CAPM, 
15 
rational investors will hold a portfolio of 
16 
stocks, generally 60 or more, such that the 
17 
overall risk of the portfolio, in terms of 
18 
variability of its returns, closely approximates 
19 
that of the market as a whole.  Thus, the only 
20 
risk that matters in the CAPM equation is said 
21 
to be systematic risk, or risk that cannot be 
22 
diversified away. 
23 
Q. 
What is systematic risk? 
24 
2426


--- Page 443 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
91 
 
A. 
Systematic risk is a risk common to all equity 
1 
securities that cannot be eliminated through 
2 
diversification.  It is measured by a variable 
3 
denominated as beta.  Systematic risk is also 
4 
known as market risk, which is the uncertainty 
5 
inherent to the entire market or market segment.  
6 
Changes in interest rates, recessions, and wars 
7 
are all examples of systematic risks. 
8 
 
Unsystematic risk, on the other hand, is risk 
9 
that is specific to a particular stock.  While 
10 
it is assumed that most stocks tend to go along 
11 
with the general market, at least to some 
12 
extent, factors that are specific to an 
13 
individual stock are said to affect its 
14 
unsystematic risk. 
15 
Q. 
How does one measure an individual stock’s value 
16 
using the CAPM? 
17 
A. 
According to the CAPM, the appropriate way to 
18 
measure an individual stock’s risk is through a 
19 
correlation of its return relative to the market 
20 
as a whole, known as beta.  A stock with a beta 
21 
of 1.0 has a return that mirrors the return of 
22 
the “market,” usually taken to mean the S&P 500, 
23 
as a whole.  Betas of less than 1.0, which are 
24 
2427


--- Page 444 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
92 
 
typical for utility stocks given the moderating 
1 
influence of regulation and the accompanying 
2 
perception of reduced risk, indicate that the 
3 
stocks are less volatile than the market as a 
4 
whole.  Therefore, the CAPM informs us that 
5 
investors will only be compensated for their 
6 
actual risk, as measured by beta.  Thus, in the 
7 
case of estimating the return requirements of 
8 
utility investors, the CAPM result will reflect 
9 
the degree to which utility stocks are less 
10 
volatile than the market as a whole. 
11 
Q. 
On what assumptions is the CAPM based? 
12 
A. 
The model is based on the assumptions that: (1) 
13 
the capital market is competitive and efficient; 
14 
(2) investors are risk-averse and demand higher 
15 
returns for higher risk; (3) market participants 
16 
can lend and borrow unlimited amounts under the 
17 
risk-free rate of interest; and, (4) investors 
18 
hold diversified portfolios and operate in 
19 
capital markets with no transaction costs, 
20 
taxes, or restrictions on financial 
21 
transactions.  Under the CAPM model, the 
22 
expected return of a security or portfolio is 
23 
equal to the rate on a risk-free security plus a 
24 
2428


--- Page 445 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
93 
 
risk premium multiplied by the asset’s 
1 
systematic risk. 
 
2 
Q. 
Please describe the methodology you used to 
3 
determine your CAPM results. 
4 
A. 
Consistent with the approach that Staff has 
5 
employed and the Commission has adopted for many 
6 
years, I averaged the results of two forms of 
7 
the CAPM: the Traditional CAPM and the Zero-Beta 
8 
CAPM. 
9 
Q. 
Why did you use two forms of the CAPM? 
10 
A. 
Research has revealed that the Traditional CAPM 
11 
model may underestimate the required return when 
12 
betas are below 1.0.  Therefore, in an effort to 
13 
avoid an underestimated return, I use a Zero-
14 
Beta methodology as well.  By averaging in the 
15 
result of the Zero-Beta approach, which is only 
16 
partially determined by the beta used, this 
17 
tendency is addressed and corrected for, and 
18 
ultimately enhances the accuracy of my overall 
19 
CAPM ROE determination. 
20 
Q. 
Please describe how a CAPM result is calculated 
21 
using the Traditional CAPM method. 
22 
A. 
The Traditional CAPM method calculates a 
23 
required return based on three inputs: the rate 
24 
2429


--- Page 446 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
94 
 
of return on a risk-free investment (Rf), the 
1 
level of systematic risk for an investment (β 
2 
for beta), and the expected market risk premium, 
3 
referred to as MRP.  Typically, the MRP is 
4 
calculated by subtracting the risk-free rate 
5 
from the expected market return (Rm).  The form 
6 
that the Traditional CAPM takes is as follows:  
7 
 
Required Return = Rf + β*(Rm-Rf) 
8 
Q. 
Please describe how a CAPM result is calculated 
9 
using the Zero-Beta CAPM method. 
10 
A. 
I used the same inputs as the Traditional CAPM 
11 
methodology; however, instead of multiplying 
12 
beta by the MRP, as shown in the calculation of 
13 
the Traditional CAPM methodology, I determined 
14 
the MRP for the proxy group by multiplying 0.75 
15 
by beta by the MRP and adding 0.25 times the 
16 
MRP.  This can be expressed as:  
17 
 
K = Rf + 0.75*β*( Rm–Rf) + ( Rm–Rf). 
18 
Q. 
How did you calculate the risk-free rate used in 
19 
this analysis? 
20 
A. 
The risk-free rate was estimated by averaging 
21 
the 10-year and 30-year Treasury bond yields for 
22 
the recent three-month period, July 2023 through 
23 
September 2023.  The result for the three-month 
24 
2430


--- Page 447 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
95 
 
period ended September 2023 is 4.19%. 
1 
Q. 
Why do you use both the 10-year and the 30-year 
2 
Treasury bond yields to estimate the risk-free 
3 
rate? 
4 
A. 
I employed the average of the 10-year and 30-
5 
year Treasury yields because utility investors 
6 
generally have both intermediate and long-term 
7 
investment horizons.  To account for these two 
8 
investment horizons, I blended the 10-year and 
9 
30-year Treasury securities to calculate my 
10 
result.  In the past, the Commission has 
11 
consistently adopted this approach.  For 
12 
instance, in the Order Establishing Rates for 
13 
Electric Service, issued June 17, 2011 in Case 
14 
10-E-0362, Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc., 
15 
the Commission stated its preference for this 
16 
approach.  Specifically, on page 75 of that 
17 
Order, the Commission noted that, “using a 
18 
combination of treasury yields is consistent 
19 
with our practice and supported by the varying 
20 
nature of investor holding periods.” 
21 
Q. 
Why are you using a three-month average of the 
22 
Treasury yields for your risk-free rate 
23 
calculation? 
24 
2431


--- Page 448 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
96 
 
A. 
I employed the three-month timeframe in my CAPM 
1 
analysis to be consistent with the three-month 
2 
timeframe used to calculate the stock prices in 
3 
my DCF analysis.  In addition, I rely on Value 
4 
Line data, which is also published every three 
5 
months.  I consider the use of a three-month 
6 
average to be appropriate because it smooths out 
7 
potential short-term volatility while also 
8 
maintaining a realistic representation of the 
9 
current movements in the market. 
10 
Q. 
How did you determine the beta for the CAPM 
11 
analyses? 
12 
A. 
I calculated the mean, or average, beta reported 
13 
by Value Line for each of the 26 companies in my 
14 
proxy group.  The mean beta of my proxy group is 
15 
0.91.  
16 
Q. 
Why did you use the mean beta rather than the 
17 
median beta of the proxy group? 
18 
A. 
As a practical matter, there is only a minor 
19 
difference, as the median beta of the proxy 
20 
group is 0.90.  Employing the median beta rather 
21 
than the mean in this case would not change my 
22 
overall ROE recommendation.  Nonetheless, Staff 
23 
began using the mean beta rather than the median 
24 
2432


--- Page 449 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
97 
 
beta in the CAPM analyses because Value Line 
1 
reports its beta determinations in increments of 
2 
0.05.  Changes in the beta on a month-to-month 
3 
basis using the median value can only result in 
4 
changes of increments of 0.05 and, therefore, do 
5 
not provide results that are as smooth as those 
6 
based on the mean.  Thus, the use of the median 
7 
beta value has the potential to introduce 
8 
unintended volatility in the determination of 
9 
the proxy group’s beta.  This is why I 
10 
determined the mean beta is the more appropriate 
11 
input to be used in the CAPM. 
12 
Q. 
How does Value Line calculate beta? 
13 
A. 
Value Line derives its beta from a regression 
14 
analysis of the relationship between weekly 
15 
percentage changes in the price of a stock and 
16 
weekly percentage changes in the New York Stock 
17 
Exchange Index over a period of five years.  
18 
With shorter price histories, a shorter time 
19 
period is used, but two years is the minimum.  
20 
Value Line also smooths the coarse betas to 
21 
reflect the theory that betas have a natural 
22 
tendency to converge to 1.0. 
23 
Q. 
Please explain how the calculation of the beta 
24 
2433


--- Page 450 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
98 
 
can be problematic. 
1 
A. 
Beta is supposed to represent the future 
2 
volatility of a given stock relative to the 
3 
market as a whole.  However, because future 
4 
volatility is an unknown, betas must be measured 
5 
on a historical basis.  The problem with using 
6 
historically derived betas is that, when the 
7 
systematic risks of a firm or industry change, 
8 
these historically derived betas may not be 
9 
reliable indicators of future volatility.  Thus, 
10 
the measurement of past stock price movements is 
11 
a poor predictor of future stock price 
12 
movements. 
13 
Q. 
Do all firms that report betas use the same 
14 
methodology? 
15 
A. 
No.  Another, and perhaps more significant, 
16 
shortcoming of beta calculations is the often-
17 
wide disparity of betas between the various 
18 
firms that report this measure.  For instance, 
19 
Staff has exclusively relied on betas reported 
20 
by Value Line for at least the past twenty 
21 
years, as they are generally calculated over a 
22 
five-year period.  This is long enough to 
23 
produce reliable estimates but recent enough to 
24 
2434


--- Page 451 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
99 
 
reflect current market conditions.  Other firms, 
1 
such as Bloomberg Professional, referred to as 
2 
Bloomberg, allow individuals to customize the 
3 
look back period of determining betas up to 10 
4 
years.  Longer time periods used to calculate a 
5 
beta can produce notable variances in the beta 
6 
results.  However, most firms typically resort 
7 
to applying shorter time periods in their 
8 
calculations of beta. 
9 
Q. 
Why are those disparities a concern?    
10 
A. 
The concern is that, depending upon the source, 
11 
betas can vary significantly, and thus produce 
12 
CAPM-based ROE results that differ substantially 
13 
solely because of the source chosen for the beta 
14 
input.  I find that the five-year time period 
15 
used by Value Line produces reliable estimates 
16 
because, as discussed above, five years is long 
17 
enough to produce reliable estimates but recent 
18 
enough to reflect current market conditions.  
19 
Q. 
How did you determine the appropriate cost of 
20 
market return to use in your CAPM analyses, and 
21 
what was the result? 
22 
A. 
Because the cost of equity is, by nature, a 
23 
forward-looking concept, I employed an ex-ante 
24 
2435


--- Page 452 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
100 
 
analysis, relying upon the required market 
1 
return estimates published monthly by Bank of 
2 
America Quantitative Profiles, or BAQP, formally 
3 
known as Merrill Lynch in its Quantitative 
4 
Profile reports.  My market rate of return is 
5 
derived from the two-forward-looking returns on 
6 
the market, the required return and the implied 
7 
return.  Specifically, I used the monthly 
8 
editions of the Quantitative Profile reports for 
9 
July 2023, August 2023, and September 2023, and 
10 
averaged the required and implied market returns 
11 
of each of the three point-in-time forecasts, to 
12 
arrive at a market return of 10.83%.  Excerpts 
13 
of these publications are included in 
14 
Exhibit__(ASH-25).  The full reports are 
15 
available upon request.   
16 
 Q. Why are you using an average of the most recent 
17 
three months of BAQP’s expected market returns 
18 
in your calculation? 
19 
A. 
Generally speaking, I used expected market 
20 
return estimates from the most recent three 
21 
months to be consistent with the timeframes of 
22 
the other data inputs used in my CAPM and DCF 
23 
calculations.  By matching the timeframe upon 
24 
2436


--- Page 453 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
101 
 
which my other parameters are calculated, I 
1 
arrive at the most appropriate estimate of the 
2 
required market rate of return to use.  It is 
3 
also critical that the timeframes used to 
4 
determine the risk-free rate of return and the 
5 
market rate of return be the same in order to 
6 
validly calculate the MRP.   
7 
Q. 
How did you derive your MRP? 
8 
A. 
Given my risk-free rate of 4.19%, I calculated 
9 
the expected MRP to be 6.64% by subtracting the 
10 
risk-free rate from the 10.83% expected market 
11 
return. 
12 
Q. 
Why did you not rely on an ex-post method to 
13 
derive the appropriate MRP? 
14 
A. 
The application of an ex-post, or historical, 
15 
market risk premium method is problematic 
16 
because ex-post MRPs are based on the premise 
17 
that past performance is a valid proxy for 
18 
expectations regarding future results.  I reject 
19 
the underlying theory of this approach because 
20 
it has never been substantiated.  The market 
21 
risk premium is essentially a mean-reverting 
22 
measure.  Furthermore, I am disinclined to 
23 
advocate such an approach because it has been 
24 
2437


--- Page 454 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
102 
 
shown to periodically produce counterintuitive 
1 
results.  In addition, this approach has also 
2 
been known to suffer from the fact that it is 
3 
highly sensitive to the actual time period 
4 
selected to calculate the premium.  For example, 
5 
from time to time, the actual market return can 
6 
become negative due to many factors, including 
7 
recessions, war, and/or depressed earnings.  
8 
COVID-19 and its aftermath is an example of 
9 
precisely this kind of event risk. 
10 
Q. 
Why did you use BAQP data to calculate the MRP 
11 
in your CAPM analyses? 
12 
A. 
The Commission has consistently applied and 
13 
implemented this market risk premium methodology 
14 
since the Opinion and Order Concerning Revenue 
15 
Requirement and Rate Design issued on October 3, 
16 
1996 in Case 95-G-1034, concerning Central 
17 
Hudson.  In that Order, the Commission approved 
18 
the use of the Merrill Lynch estimate stating, 
19 
“…the Judge's market return calculation based on 
20 
Merrill Lynch data is a reasonable method of 
21 
deriving a risk premium.” 
22 
Q. 
Can you please explain how the calculation of 
23 
the MRP can be problematic?  
24 
2438


--- Page 455 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
103 
 
A. 
My concern regarding the calculation of the MRP 
1 
is that the expected MRP is unknown and, because 
2 
it is unknown, many adherents to this 
3 
methodology advocate the use of an ex-post MRP.  
4 
The view of these individuals is that the MRP is 
5 
essentially a mean-reverting time series, which 
6 
may be volatile over the short-run, but over the 
7 
long-run exhibits a stable long-run average. 
8 
 
As stated earlier, I do not employ a 
9 
historically derived MRP specifically because it 
10 
inherently does not reflect current economic 
11 
conditions, and it lacks the ability to capture 
12 
and reflect ongoing structural shifts in the 
13 
economy.  I acknowledge that using an expected 
14 
MRP in my CAPM analysis is, by necessity, 
15 
subject to a substantial amount of judgment.  
16 
That is among the principal reasons that Staff 
17 
has consistently advocated that the CAPM should 
18 
only be accorded half the weight of the DCF-
19 
derived cost of equity estimates.  
20

18

19 
A.

20 
that remains after accounting for all the 
21 
expenses necessary to keep the business running.  
22 
It is not uncommon for competitive ventures to 
23 
be unprofitable at times, meaning that operating 
24 
2450


--- Page 467 ---

Hale

income could be very small, or even negative.  
1 
In such an instance, this venture could appear 
2

5 
company’s main line of business.  Consider, for 
6 
instance, a holding company that receives 20% of 
7 
its revenues from utility sources and 80% from

MR. NOWAK’S DCF METHODOLOGY 
21 
Q. 
Please summarize Mr. Nowak’s DCF methodology. 
22 
A. 
Mr. Nowak calculated three multi-stage DCF 
23 
calculations, based upon three different growth 
24 
2457


--- Page 474 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
122 
 
scenarios, in order to develop his DCF range of 
1 
9.29% to 10.45%.  As mentioned earlier, these 
2 
scenarios include low, mean, and high estimated 
3 
earnings growth rates from Value Line, Yahoo! 
4 
Finance, and Zack’s. 
5 
Q. 
Please briefly describe the three stages of Mr. 
6 
Nowak’s multi-stage DCF models. 
7 
A. 
The first stage of Mr. Nowak’s multi-stage DCF 
8 
models is calculated using short-term earnings 
9 
per share growth estimates from Value Line, 
10 
Yahoo! Finance, and Zack’s.  These published 
11 
forecasts inform three different growth rate 
12 
scenarios.  One scenario is based on the lowest 
13 
of these forecasts, another is based on the 
14 
highest, and a third is based on the mean of 
15 
these estimates.  This first stage represents 
16 
years one through five of his DCF model.   
17 
 
 
Mr. Nowak’s second stage is a blend of his 
18 
first and third stage growth rates for each 
19 
company in his proxy group.  The second stage 
20 
gradually converges towards Mr. Nowak’s 
21 
forecasted long-term GDP growth rate as he 
22 
levels out the difference over a five-year 
23 
period.  His second stage represents years six 
24 
2458


--- Page 475 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
123 
 
through ten of his DCF model.   
1 
 
 
Mr. Nowak’s third stage uses a long-term 
2 
GDP growth rate that is derived from forecasted 
3 
inflation rates in conjunction with historic 
4 
economic growth rates.  His third stage starts 
5 
at year eleven and continues into perpetuity. 
6 
Q. 
Please describe your core concerns about Mr. 
7 
Nowak’s DCF models. 
8 
A. 
I am concerned about Mr. Nowak’s application of 
9 
1) earnings growth rates rather than dividend 
10 
growth rates for the short-term; 2) the use of 
11 
excessive growth rates; and 3) the assumed 
12 
timing of future dividend payments. 
13 
Q. 
Please explain why Mr. Nowak’s use of earnings 
14 
rather than dividends to estimate the short-term 
15 
growth rates in his DCF model is problematic. 
16 
A. 
Mr. Nowak’s DCF model uses earnings growth rate 
17 
forecasts published by analysts in order to 
18 
estimate short-term growth in dividends.  This 
19 
approach assumes that growth in dividends will 
20 
approximate growth in earnings, although Mr. 
21 
Nowak did not provide any evidence demonstrating 
22 
this assumption is valid.  Regardless, Mr. 
23 
Nowak’s assumption is unnecessary, given that 
24 
2459


--- Page 476 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
124 
 
dividend growth rates or forecasts are readily 
1 
available and predictable.  This approach also 
2 
stands in direct contrast to the basic premise 
3 
of the DCF, which measures the present value of 
4 
future dividends, not earnings. 
5 
Q. 
How does Mr. Nowak’s use of earnings growth 
6 
instead of dividend growth lead to inaccuracy in 
7 
his DCF model? 
8 
A. 
Mr. Nowak’s application of earnings growth in 
9 
his DCF model fails to properly account for the 
10 
division of annual earnings into retained 
11 
earnings and dividends.  Investors recognize 
12 
that annual earnings represent a company’s 
13 
profit before the consideration of its dividend 
14 
payout and the use of retained earnings.  
15 
Retained earnings provide a company’s management 
16 
with the flexibility to maintain its current 
17 
dividends should it suffer a loss of revenue or 
18 
unplanned expenses, effectively providing a 
19 
cushion for investors when profitability 
20 
shrinks.  In fact, a company’s dividend policy 
21 
is one of the ways that it insulates 
22 
shareholders from the volatility of earnings 
23 
from one time period to the next.  Indeed, 
24 
2460


--- Page 477 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
125 
 
investors generally prefer dividends to be 
1 
steadily increasing and predictably regular.  
2 
Thus, Mr. Nowak’s sole reliance on earnings 
3 
growth in his DCF model rather than dividend 
4 
growth reflects neither realistic growth rates 
5 
nor typical corporate practice. 
6 
Q. 
Are there additional reasons that make Mr. 
7 
Nowak’s application of earnings growth, rather 
8 
than dividend growth, problematic? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  Mr. Nowak’s model does not account for the 
10 
issuance of common equity shares in the future.  
11 
My DCF model is more comprehensive because it 
12 
uses actual forecasted dividend growth rates, 
13 
and accounts for changes in the number of common 
14 
equity shares. 
15 
 
 
Furthermore, it is highly unlikely that 
16 
investors would rely exclusively on the earnings 
17 
growth rate forecasts of Wall Street analysts in 
18 
determining short-term dividend projections.  On 
19 
page 47 of his testimony, Mr. Nowak cites the 
20 
1986 Robert S. Harris article, “Using Analysts’ 
21 
Growth Forecasts to Estimate Shareholder 
22 
Required Rates of Return” and asserts that “a 
23 
growing body of knowledge shows that analysts’ 
24 
2461


--- Page 478 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
126 
 
earnings forecasts are indeed reflected in the 
1 
stock prices.”  I agree that the company’s stock 
2 
price does indeed reflect published earnings 
3 
forecasts from analysts, insofar as all relevant 
4 
information is incorporated into a company’s 
5 
stock price.  However, forecasted earnings are 
6 
far from the only, or even the most important, 
7 
pieces of information considered by investors 
8 
and reflected in the stock price.  Corporations 
9 
create value by using their earnings in a 
10 
variety of ways, the most important of which, by 
11 
far, is the payment of cash dividends.  Given 
12 
the importance of dividends to shareholder 
13 
value, I would expect investors to be especially 
14 
concerned with the prospect of future dividends.  
15 
Furthermore, Mr. Nowak has not provided any 
16 
basis to support a proportional relationship 
17 
between earnings growth and dividend growth 
18 
assumed by his model.  Dividend payout policies 
19 
are based on many factors, including individual 
20 
company cash flow requirements, current market 
21 
conditions, future economic expectations, and 
22 
maintaining investor confidence in the company.  
23 
Rational investors will not look at forecasted 
24 
2462


--- Page 479 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
127 
 
earnings alone in a vacuum.  Rather, they will 
1 
factor in all relevant information when 
2 
considering an investment.  The simple fact 
3 
remains that earnings, even in the relatively 
4 
stable utility industry, can be very difficult 
5 
to predict because of the impact of 
6 
unpredictable events.  For instance, many 
7 
earnings forecasts in the early part of the last 
8 
decade turned out to be wide of the mark because 
9 
of difficulties in forecasting the course of 
10 
deregulation and the extent of competition.  For 
11 
these reasons, I find that dividends are far 
12 
more predictable than earnings, and thus 
13 
dividend forecasts are more likely to be 
14 
accurate as compared to earnings forecasts.  
15

This is particularly important to the major

MR. NOWAK’S CAPM METHODOLOGY 
20 
Q. 
Would you please summarize Mr. Nowak’s CAPM 
21 
methodology? 
22 
A. 
Mr. Nowak calculated six ROE estimates using the 
23 
Traditional CAPM and six ROE estimates using the 
24 
2468


--- Page 485 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
133 
 
Zero-Beta CAPM for a total of twelve CAPM-
1 
derived ROE estimates.  The CAPM models applied 
2 
both Value Line and Bloomberg beta sources for 
3 
the companies in his proxy group with three 
4 
different risk-free rate estimates of 3.77%, 
5 
3.78%, and 3.80%, which will be discussed in 
6 
further detail below.  Mr. Nowak applied the 
7 
same market rate of return estimate of 12.12% to 
8 
all twelve of his CAPM models. 
9 
Q. 
Please explain how Mr. Nowak derived the risk-
10 
free rate estimates that he used in his CAPM 
11 
calculations. 
12 
A. 
Mr. Nowak used three different risk-free rate 
13

21

22

24

--- Page 489 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419

1

of the distribution for historic betas contain

4

Q. 
Please explain how Mr. Nowak derived the market 
10

11 
A. 
Mr. Nowak’s market rate of return has two 
12

13 
derived an expected return on the S&P 500 equal 
14 
to 13.46%.  In doing so, he calculated the 
15

16

17 
which he found equal to 1.62%.  For the purposes 
18 
of this calculation, Mr. Nowak included non-
19

20

21

22

earnings growth rate estimates of the companies 
24 
2473


--- Page 490 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
138 
 
in the S&P 500, weighted by market 
1 
capitalization, which he found equal to 11.74%.  
2 
Adding the weighted average dividend yield to 
3 
the weighted average of earnings growth resulted 
4 
in a total return estimate of 13.36%.  The ten-
5 
basis point difference between this figure and 
6 
the 13.46% that Mr. Nowak shows in his MRP 
7 
analyses is attributed to his dividing his long-
8 
term growth rate estimate of 11.74% by two and 
9 
adding one, which equates to 1.06.  Then, he 
10 
multiplied the 1.06 by his average dividend 
11 
yield of 1.62%, which equaled 1.72%.  Lastly, he 
12 
added 1.72% to the long-term growth rate 
13 
estimate of 11.74%, which brought him to his 
14 
13.46% market return.

For the calculation of the second component

MR. NOWAK’S WEIGHTING OF DCF AND CAPM RESULTS 
23 
Q. 
Why does Mr. Nowak argue that the CAPM 
24 
2477


--- Page 494 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
142 
 
methodology should be accorded an equal 
1 
weighting with the DCF methodology? 
2 
A. 
Mr. Nowak contends that DCF models are currently 
3 
producing results that understate the true cost 
4 
of equity.  On page 51 of his testimony, Mr. 
5 
Nowak claims that “…it is important to recognize 
6 
that the results of the DCF model are likely 
7 
understated.” 
8 
Q. 
Do you agree with Mr. Nowak’s inclusion of 
9 
results that place equal emphasis on the CAPM 
10 
methodology?  
11 
A. 
No.  The CAPM methodology has two primary inputs 
12 
which generate controversy, whereas the DCF has 
13 
only one subjective input.  It is primarily for 
14 
this reason the Commission has consistently 
15 
found the DCF methodology to be the best tool 
16 
for estimating the ROE.  As I noted earlier, in 
17 
the Rate Order issued April 24, 2009 in Case 08-
18 
E-0539, CECONY, the Commission stated that among 
19 
the reasons it accords a two-thirds weighting of 
20 
the DCF methodology is that “the DCF relies on 
21 
readily available data to make objective 
22 
estimates of investors’ return requirements.” 
23 
Q. 
How do you respond to Mr. Nowak’s contention 
24 
2478


--- Page 495 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
143 
 
that the DCF model is likely underestimating the 
1 
true cost of equity estimates? 
2 
A. 
As I have demonstrated throughout this 
3 
testimony, my DCF model employs reasonable and 
4 
timely inputs and assumptions and, thus, my DCF 
5 
results are not understated.  The primary reason 
6 
why Mr. Nowak’s DCF results are lower than his 
7 
CAPM results is because he chose to employ an 
8 
excessive market return in his CAPM analyses, as 
9 
well as excessive forecasts for the risk-free 
10 
rate.  Thus, it is not surprising that his CAPM 
11 
analyses yield such different ROE results than 
12 
his DCF analyses.  For example, Mr. Nowak’s 
13 
proxy group has an average CAPM result of 
14 
10.97%.  This is 113 basis points higher than 
15 
the average DCF for his proxy group of 9.84%.  
16 
This difference is more of a reflection of his 
17 
chosen inputs as opposed to any imperfections in 
18 
the DCF or CAPM theories. 
19 
Q. 
Why should the CAPM be given less weight than 
20 
the DCF in the weighting of the ROE 
21 
methodologies? 
22 
A. 
The CAPM presents a conceptual framework that 
23 
provides a reasonable estimate of a firm’s cost 
24 
2479


--- Page 496 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
144 
 
of equity.  However, given some of the 
1 
weaknesses of the CAPM discussed previously in 
2 
my testimony, I recommend that the Commission 
3 
continue the CAPM weighting of one-third and the 
4 
DCF weighting of two-thirds. 
5 
Q. 
Has the Commission ever addressed the issue of 
6 
the proper weighting of the DCF and CAPM? 
7 
A. 
Yes.  As I specifically noted earlier, in both 
8 
the Rate Order issued April 20, 2017 in Case 16-
9 
G-0257, National Fuel Gas Distribution Corp, and 
10 
the Order Setting Permanent Rates, Reconciling 
11 
Overpayments During Temporary Rate Period, and 
12 
Establishing Disposition of Property Tax 
13 
Refunds, issued October 18, 2007 in Case 06-E-
14 
1433 regarding O&R, the Commission stated its 
15 
preference for applying a two-thirds weighting 
16 
of the DCF and a one-third weighting of the CAPM 
17 
in the determination of the cost of equity.  
18 
Q. 
Are there other reasons that the CAPM should be 
19 
given less weight in determining the recommended 
20 
ROE? 
21 
A. 
Yes.  The CAPM should be given less weight 
22 
relative to the DCF because the CAPM components 
23 
are less observable and are more dependent on 
24 
2480


--- Page 497 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419   
Hale 
 
 
145 
 
estimations.  In contrast, the DCF model inputs 
1 
are readily observable outside of the dividend 
2 
growth rates.  The use of fewer subjective 
3 
inputs in the DCF relative to the CAPM provides 
4 
a more stable foundation, and thus a lesser 
5 
chance of error in a ROE calculation.  The 
6 
Commission has recognized the strength of the 
7 
DCF model by its repeated reliance on the two-
8 
thirds weighting in numerous rate proceedings.  
9 
While the CAPM presents a conceptual framework 
10 
that provides a reasonable estimate of a 
11 
company’s cost of equity, the CAPM weaknesses 
12 
provide a less stable foundation in the 
13 
calculation of the return of equity.  Given some 
14 
of the CAPM’s shortcomings as already discussed 
15 
in my testimony, the Commission should continue 
16 
to accord the CAPM methodology a one-third 
17 
weighting.  I find that Mr. Nowak has not 
18 
presented any persuasive evidence that would 
19 
compel the Commission to reject its long-
20 
standing policy of the two-thirds and one-third 
21 
weighting of the DCF and CAPM, respectively.  
22 
Q. 
Does this conclude your testimony at this time? 
23 
A. 
Yes, it does.

ongoing programs? 
13 
A. 
The core mission of the Department is to ensure 
14 
affordable, safe, secure, and reliable access to 
15 
electric, gas, steam, telecommunications, and 
16 
water services for New York State’s residential 
17 
and business consumers, at just and reasonable 
18 
rates, while protecting the natural environment.  
19 
Staff’s role, in the context of reviewing 
20 
utility capital projects, is focused on ensuring 
21 
that forecasted projects are in the best 
22 
interest of customers, such that they are 
23 
necessary from an electric safety, reliability, 
24 
2543


--- Page 560 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
15 
 
and resiliency perspective, while also taking 
1 
into consideration the costs associated with 
2 
such projects and programs and their respective 
3 
impacts on customers.  A utility rate plan, 
4 
whether in the context of a litigated rate case 
5 
or a negotiated settlement, includes forecasted 
6 
capital costs for specific, utility-identified 
7 
programs, upon which customer rates will be 
8 
derived.  If a utility’s capital project 
9 
forecasts are not transparent in the context of 
10 
its historical programs, it creates unnecessary 
11 
difficulty in understanding and comparing 
12 
historic actual versus future forecasted 
13 
expenditures and, therefore, determining whether 
14 
future capital spending is just and reasonable.  
15 
This is not only true for Staff, but it is 
16 
likely more difficult for stakeholders to 
17 
understand.  Additionally, changing the method 
18 
of forecasting capital programs between rate 
19 
proceedings creates more layers of difficulty 
20 
for a utility, Staff, and stakeholders when 
21 
trying to track historic and forecasted actual 
22 
and budgeted costs. 
23

A. 
No.  Generally, when utilities file for rate 
1 
increases, they also file their responses to a 
2 
list of pre-filed IRs that are developed and 
3 
updated by Staff.  The intent of these pre-filed 
4 
IRs is two-fold.  First, utility responses to 
5 
these pre-filed IRs are intended to provide 
6 
Staff the necessary data and information to 
7 
begin its review of the portion of the Company’s 
8 
rate request that is within their purview.  
9 
Having access to these IR responses at the date 
10 
of the Company’s rate filing allows Staff to 
11 
begin its review immediately.  Second, the list 
12 
of pre-filed IRs is also beneficial to the 
13 
Company in that it allows the Company to know 
14 
and understand the information, and format of 
15 
this information, that Staff requires to begin 
16 
its review.  Additionally, the list of pre-filed 
17 
IRs removes the burden of what would otherwise 
18 
be an immediate influx of hundreds of IRs from 
19 
Staff at the onset of the case.  Simply put, the 
20 
generation of a list of pre-filed IRs is 
21 
beneficial to both Staff and the utilities.

Q. 
Describe the purpose of Central Hudson’s Grid 
6 
Mod program. 
7 
A. 
The purpose of the Grid Mod program is to 
8 
improve the reliability, resiliency, and 
9 
efficiency of the electric distribution system.  
10 
This includes but is not limited to the 
11 
installation of assets that promote automation, 
12 
maintain optimum voltage levels, and provide 
13 
real-time information on the state of the 
14 
distribution system.  Some of the projects that 
15 
fall under the Grid Mod program include the 
16 
Distribution Management System (DMS), 
17 
Distribution Automation (DA), Network Strategy 
18 
communication system, and an Energy Management 
19 
System (EMS) projects.  
20 
Q. 
How has Grid Mod changed the operating 
21 
procedures for Distribution Operators? 
22 
A. 
Distribution Operators are now able to establish 
23 
electric clearances and safe work zones for 
24 
2583


--- Page 600 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
55 
 
field crews, assess telemetry received from 
1 
field devices, and make decisions to safely 
2 
restore customers.  The ADMS, as stated on page 
3 
60 of the Company’s ECOP testimony, is an 
4 
integrated software system to monitor and 
5 
control field Intelligent Electronic Devices, or 
6 
IEDs.  The software is designed to stabilize and 
7 
optimize power flow of the distribution grid and 
8 
in the event of a fault, advise potential 
9 
faulted locations as well as recommendations to 
10 
restore power.  This system will recognize 
11 
status changes in the field and present 
12 
Distribution Operators with multiple restoration 
13 
solutions that will be reviewed before 
14 
implementation.  Full automation will be 
15 
deployed in the future on capable distribution 
16 
circuits after testing and user experience.

6 
Major Storm Reserve 
7 
Q. 
What is a Major Storm Reserve? 
8 
A. 
Storm restoration must be carried out for 
9 
continuity of electric service, safety, and 
10 
reliability.  Restoration efforts after a major 
11 
storm occurs, and the associated costs, can be 
12 
significant and cannot be adequately forecasted 
13 
in advance.  A Major Storm Reserve is an 
14 
accounting mechanism that allows a company to 
15 
collect an allowance through its rates for storm 
16 
restoration efforts but defer the variance 
17 
between that allowance and restoration costs 
18 
actually incurred.  That variance, either a 
19 
regulatory asset or a regulatory liability, can 
20 
be collected from or returned to customers as 
21 
part of a company’s future rate filing.  Storm 
22 
costs charged to the Major Storm Reserve are 
23 
reviewed by Staff to verify the costs are 
24 
2601


--- Page 618 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
73 
 
accurate, and in compliance with the Order which 
1 
allows the company to use reserve accounting for 
2 
major storms.   
3 
Q. 
Describe Central Hudson’s existing Major Storm 
4 
Reserve. 
5 
A. 
Appendix U of the Joint Proposal adopted in the 
6 
2020 Rate Case details conditions surrounding 
7 
the use of the Major Storm Reserve.  The Joint 
8 
Proposal included $4.674 million in funding for 
9 
the Major Storm Reserve for each rate year of 
10 
that rate plan.  The reserve balance, whether a 
11 
debt or credit balance, accrues carrying charges 
12 
at the Company’s pre-tax rate of return.  Once a 
13 
storm has been determined to fall within the 
14 
Commission’s definition of a major storm, 
15 
incremental restoration costs incurred as a 
16 
result of the adverse weather event must reach a 
17 
level of at least $500,000 in order for expenses 
18 
related to the event to be chargeable to the 
19 
Major Storm Reserve.  Central Hudson is required 
20 
to file data demonstrating that the event 
21 
qualified as a major storm, along with 
22 
documentation supporting the restoration costs 
23 
to the Office of Accounting, Audits, and Finance 
24 
2602


--- Page 619 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
74 
 
within 120 days of the date on which the Company 
1 
was able to restore service to all customers.  
2 
Additionally, the Company is allowed to record 
3 
certain pre-staging event costs to the Major 
4 
Storm Reserve, as we will discuss in more detail 
5 
later. 
6 
Q. 
Has Central Hudson proposed any changes to its 
7 
Major Storm Reserve accounting? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  As outlined on pages 82-83 of the ECOP’s 
9 
direct testimony, the Company is requesting 
10 
three changes to its Major Storm Reserve.  
11 
First, the Company proposes a change to the pre-
12 
staging event thresholds as defined in Appendix 
13 
U of the Joint Proposal adopted in the 2020 Rate 
14 
Case.  Second, the Company proposes an increase 
15 
of $10.2 million to the annual rate allowance 
16 
for the Major Storm Reserve, bringing the Rate 
17 
Year allowance to $14.80 million per year.  
18 
Third, the Company proposes to amortize its 
19 
exiting Major Storm Reserve regulatory asset 
20 
over ten years and collect $4.056 million per 
21 
year, beginning in the rate year.  The Major 
22 
Storm Reserve annual rate allowance and the 
23 
Major Storm regulatory asset proposals and 
24 
2603


--- Page 620 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
75 
 
calculations are addressed in the testimony of 
1 
the Staff Accounting Panel.   
2 
Q.  Define pre-staging and mobilization costs. 
3 
A. 
Pre-staging and mobilization costs are those 
4 
costs incurred by the Company when additional 
5 
crews are brought into the utility’s service 
6 
territory in anticipation that a storm would 
7 
significantly impact routine electric 
8 
operations.  Central Hudson is authorized to 
9 
charge these costs to the Major Storm Reserve 
10 
when incurred in reasonable anticipation that a 
11 
storm will affect its electric operations to the 
12 
degree of meeting the criteria of a major storm, 
13 
but which ultimately does not do so.  
14 
Q. 
Generally, how does Central Hudson currently 
15 
recover pre-staging costs? 
16 
A. 
According to Appendix U of the Joint Proposal 
17 
adopted in the 2020 Rate Case, pre-staging costs 
18 
can be charged to the Major Storm Reserve when a 
19 
storm event did not meet the Commission’s 
20 
definition of a major storm.  Specifically, the 
21 
Company can charge pre-staging events with 
22 
incremental costs between $100,000 and $1.75 
23 
million to the Major Storm Reserve.  Events with 
24 
2604


--- Page 621 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
76 
 
incremental restoration costs greater than $1.75 
1 
million were allowed to be charged 85 percent to 
2 
the Major Storm Reserve and 15 percent to 
3 
expense.  Central Hudson is allowed to file a 
4 
deferral petition requesting recovery of the 15 
5 
percent if certain criteria were met. 
6

16 
Q. 
Has Central Hudson proposed any other changes to 
17 
its Major Storm Reserve? 
18 
A. 
Yes.  Central Hudson requests that the 
19 
definition of a major storm be modified to be 
20 
consistent with the exclusion criteria outlined 
21 
in its reliability performance mechanisms.   
22 
Q. 
What is the current definition for a major 
23 
storm? 
24 
2607


--- Page 624 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
79 
 
A. 
A major storm is defined in Section 97.1 of the 
1 
Commission’s rules and regulations, Title 16 of 
2 
the Codes, Rules and Regulations of the State of 
3 
New York, or NYCRR, as “a period of adverse 
4 
weather during which service interruptions 
5 
affect at least 10 percent of the customers in 
6 
an operating area and/or result in customers 
7 
being without electric service for durations of 
8 
at least 24 hours.”  It should be noted that 
9 
Section 97.5 of 16 NYCRR requires utilities 
10 
provide interruption information separated into 
11 
ten cause codes.  Major storm is cause code one.  
12 
These cause codes are used to track reliability 
13 
performance and compare individual utility 
14 
performance both with and without major storms. 
15 
Q. 
What is Central Hudson’s proposed revision to 
16 
the major storm definition? 
17 
A. 
Central Hudson proposes adding the following to 
18 
the definition: “an incident resulting from a 
19 
catastrophic event beyond the control of the 
20 
Company; or an incident where problems beyond 
21 
the Company’s control involving generation or 
22 
the bulk transmission system is a key factor in 
23 
the outage.”   
24 
2608


--- Page 625 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
80 
 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree that the major storm 
1 
definition should be changed? 
2 
A. 
No.  The Commission’s major storm definition and 
3 
reliability metrics are based on the Institute 
4 
of Electrical and Electronics Engineers’ Guide 
5 
for Electric Power Distribution Reliability 
6 
Indices, or IEEE-1366.  The IEEE works to 
7 
educate and standardize the electrical and 
8 
electronic industries.  The initial purpose of 
9 
using IEEE-1366 was to promote uniformity in the 
10 
development of distribution service reliability 
11 
indices and to aid in consistent reporting 
12 
across the industry and New York State.  
13 
Changing the major storm definition just for 
14 
Central Hudson would have far reaching negative 
15 
effects for tracking historical reliability 
16 
performance and gauging the effectiveness of a 
17 
utility’s performance during storms and non-
18 
storm operations.  Therefore, the Panel does not 
19 
support changing the major storm definition. 
20 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any additional comments 
21 
regarding Central Hudson’s proposal to modify 
22 
the definition of a major storm? 
23 
A. 
Yes.  The Panel notes that there is already a 
24 
2609


--- Page 626 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
81 
 
process available where the Company may petition 
1 
the Commission directly if the Company believes 
2 
extraordinary circumstances, such as those 
3 
referenced by the Company, exist which warrant 
4 
excluding a particular outage from reliability 
5 
statistics.  The Commission affirmed this 
6 
process in its Order issued October 12, 2004, in 
7 
Case 02-E-1240, stating, “Extraordinary 
8 
circumstances can already be addressed through 
9 
the provisions for requesting a waiver under the 
10 
standards, and a more liberal listing of events 
11 
that qualify as extraordinary would not improve 
12 
upon implementation of the waiver process. A 
13 
case-by-case approach to those waivers is more 
14 
appropriate than a listing that might be overly 
15 
expansive or overly-constraining.” 
16 
Electric Reliability Metrics 
17 
Q. 
Describe Central Hudson’s existing electric 
18 
Reliability Performance Mechanism, or RPM. 
19 
A. 
Central Hudson is operating under an RPM 
20 
established in Case 09-E-0588, and modified in 
21 
Cases 12-M-0192, 14-E-0318, and 20-E-0428.  The 
22 
RPM has targets in place for the System Average 
23 
Interruption Frequency Index, or SAIFI, and the 
24 
2610


--- Page 627 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
82 
 
Customer Average Interruption Duration Index, or 
1 
CAIDI, metrics.  Each metric is used to monitor 
2 
the Company’s performance in a calendar year.  
3 
SAIFI is the number of times the average 
4 
customer’s service is interrupted in a year, and 
5 
CAIDI is the average number of hours required to 
6 
restore service to a customer whose service was 
7 
interrupted.   
8 
Q. 
Describe the electric service annual metrics 
9 
modified as part of Case 20-E-0428. 
10 
A. 
The electric service annual metrics, as defined 
11 
in Appendix Q of the Joint Proposal adopted by 
12 
the Commission in the 2020 Rate Case, are as 
13 
follows: SAIFI shall be a pre-tax, 30-basis 
14 
point potential electric negative revenue 
15 
adjustment, or NRA, for failure to achieve an 
16 
annual SAIFI target of 1.32 in 2021, 1.32 in 
17 
2022, 1.30 in 2023 and 1.30 in 2024.  CAIDI is a 
18 
pre-tax, 30-basis point potential electric NRA 
19 
for failure to achieve an annual CAIDI of 2.50 
20 
hours in 2021 through 2024.   
21 
Q. 
Did the Company propose any changes to the 
22 
existing electric RPMs? 
23 
2611

Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP

2 
1.30, and CAIDI target of 2.50 hours.  However, 
3 
the Company introduced a new two-tier target for 
4

5 
an annual system-wide SAIFI target of 1.30 would 
6 
be 10 basis points, and the NRA for failure to 
7

8 
1.34 would be 20 basis points. 
9

10

11

12

13 
would provide the Company with a financial 
14 
incentive to improve reliability performance 
15 
while maintaining target levels that are aligned 
16 
with historical results.  The Company also 
17 
believes the tier one target of 1.30 is 
18 
aggressive based on its average SAIFI 
19 
performance from 2018 through 2022.  The tier 
20 
two target of 1.34 aligns with the Company’s 
21

22

23

24 
2612

17

targeted capital improvement projects.  In

3

4

5

7

14

16

Other Deferrals 
20 
Q.

21

22 
Concerning Electric Utility Climate 
23

Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Staff EIOP 
 
 
88 
 
A.

1

2

3 
which requires utilities to submit: 1) a climate 
4

6

7 
procedures to better understand the electric 
8

9

10 
plan, or Resilience Plan, 60 days thereafter.  
11 
On June 6, 2022, the Commission issued an order 
12 
initiating Case 22-E-0222 to address such 
13 
studies. 
14 
Q.

15

16

17

18

19

20 
Study, and the associated Climate Change 
21

22
